Abstract. A famous conjecture of Artin asserts that any integer a a that is neither − 1 -1 nor a square should be a primitive root (mod p p ) for a positive proportion of primes p p . Moreover, using a heuristic argument, Artin guessed an explicit formula for the proportion; this formula is well-supported by computations and is known to hold on a generalized Riemann hypothesis, but remains open.
In this paper we propose several conjectures that capture the finer properties of the distribution of the order of a a (mod p p ) as p p varies over primes; these assertions contain Artin’s original conjecture as a special case. We prove these conjectures assuming the generalized Riemann hypothesis, as well as weaker versions unconditionally. 1. Introduction Given an integer a a and a prime p p , let ord p ( a ) \mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a) denote the multiplicative order of a a modulo p p . Exploring the distribution of ord p ( a ) \mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a) , for a a fixed as p p varies, is a classical subject: in 1927, Emil Artin proposed a heuristic argument that for any fixed integer a ≠ − 1 a\neq-1 that is not a perfect square, ord p ( a ) = p − 1 \mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)=p-1 for a positive proportion of primes p p .
His key insight was that this problem is most naturally viewed through the lens of algebraic number theory.
Artin knew that ord p ( a ) = p − 1 \mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)=p-1 if and only if p p fails to split completely in the cyclotomic-Kummer extension ℚ ⁡ ( a q , ζ q ) {\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[q]{a},\zeta_{q}) for all primes q q .
On the other hand, the prime ideal theorem implies that the density of those primes that split completely in ℚ ⁡ ( a q , ζ q ) {\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[q]{a},\zeta_{q}) is 1 / [ ℚ ( a q , ζ q ) : ℚ ] = 1 / q ( q − 1 ) {1}/{[{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[q]{a},\zeta_{q}):{\mathbb{Q}}]}={1}/{q(q-1)} .
Making the seemingly innocuous assumption that the events “ p p does not split completely in ℚ ⁡ ( a q , ζ q ) {\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[q]{a},\zeta_{q}) ” are independent, Artin predicted that the proportion of primes p p for which a a is a primitive root should be ∏ q ​ prime ( 1 − 1 q ⁡ ( q − 1 ) ) ≈ 0.3739558 , \prod_{q\text{ prime}}\left(1-\frac{1}{q(q-1)}\right)\approx 0.3739558, which is now known as Artin’s constant. Extensive computational work by Emma Lehmer and Derrick H. Lehmer provided evidence that Artin’s conjecture was correct for some values of a a but not for others, prompting Artin to write
“I was careless but the machine caught up with me.”
He realized what was going wrong in his original heuristic: for all prime a ≡ 1 ​ (mod 4 ) a\equiv 1{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $4$)}} we have a ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ a ) \sqrt{a}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{a}) , whence ℚ ⁡ ( a 2 , ζ 2 ) ⊆ ℚ ⁡ ( a a , ζ a ) {\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[2]{a},\zeta_{2})\subseteq{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[a]{a},\zeta_{a}) . In particular, any p p that does not split in ℚ ⁡ ( a 2 , ζ 2 ) {\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[2]{a},\zeta_{2}) cannot split in ℚ ⁡ ( a a , ζ a ) {\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[a]{a},\zeta_{a}) either, so the events “ p p does not split completely in ℚ ⁡ ( a q , ζ q ) {\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[q]{a},\zeta_{q}) ” are not independent after all. Artin predicted that there were no other dependences among splitting in the fields ℚ ⁡ ( a q , ζ q ) {\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[q]{a},\zeta_{q}) whenever a a is prime.
In the preface to his collected works, Artin’s former students Lang and Tate wrote down a modified version of Artin’s conjecture
(working independently, Heilbronn made the same conjecture in 1968).
To state the corrected version of Artin’s conjecture, we require the following notation: Definition 1.1 . For any nonzero rational number g g , define s ⁡ ( g ) s(g) to be the squarefree kernel of g g , which is the sign of g g times the product of all primes dividing g g to an odd power, or equivalently the unique squarefee integer such that g ​ s ​ ( g ) gs(g) is the square of a rational number, or equivalently the unique squarefee integer such that ℚ ⁡ ( g ) = ℚ ⁡ ( s ⁡ ( g ) ) {\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{g})={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{s(g)}) . Also define 𝔡 ⁡ ( g ) \mathfrak{d}(g) to be the discriminant of ℚ ⁡ ( g ) {\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{g}) , so that 𝔡 ⁡ ( g ) = { s ⁡ ( g ) , if ​ s ​ ( g ) ≡ 1 ​ (mod 4 ) , 4 ​ s ​ ( g ) , otherwise. \mathfrak{d}(g)=\begin{cases}s(g),&\text{if }s(g)\equiv 1{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $4$)}},\\
4s(g),&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases} Artin’s conjecture, which can be stated for rational numbers as well (since there are only finitely many primes for which the multiplicative order is not defined), was proved by Hooley [ 6 ] under the assumption of the generalized Riemann hypothesis for the Dedekind zeta functions of the cyclotomic-Kummer extensions mentioned earlier. It takes the following form if we assume that a a is not a perfect power (see Theorem 5.1 below for a more general version): Theorem 1.2 . Assume GRH. If a ∈ ℚ a\in{\mathbb{Q}} is not a perfect power, then (1.1) 1 π ⁡ ( x ) ​ # ​ { p ≤ x : a ​ is a primitive root (mod p ) } ∼ F a ​ ∏ q ( 1 − 1 q ⁡ ( q − 1 ) ) , \frac{1}{\pi(x)}\#\{p\leq x\colon a\text{ is a primitive root (mod $p$)}\}\sim F_{a}\prod_{q}\biggl(1-\frac{1}{q(q-1)}\biggr), where F a = { 1 , if ​ 𝔡 ​ ( a ) ≢ 1 ​ (mod 4 ) , 1 − ∏ q | 𝔡 ⁡ ( a ) − 1 q 2 − q − 1 , if ​ 𝔡 ​ ( a ) ≡ 1 ​ (mod 4 ) . F_{a}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }\mathfrak{d}(a)\not\equiv 1{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $4$)}},\\
\displaystyle 1-\prod\limits_{q\mid\mathfrak{d}(a)}\frac{-1}{q^{2}-q-1},&\text{if }\mathfrak{d}(a)\equiv 1{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $4$)}}.\end{cases} Remark 1.3 . A different expression for the density on the right-hand side of equation ( 1.1 ), which is simpler to write down but harder to use, is (1.2) ∑ ℓ = 1 ∞ μ ⁡ ( ℓ ) [ ℚ ( a ℓ , ζ ℓ ) ] : ℚ ] \sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu(\ell)}{[{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[\ell]{a},\zeta_{\ell})]:{\mathbb{Q}}]} Our goal in the present work is to formulate several conjectures which subsume Artin’s conjecture and produce predictions about finer aspects of the distribution of ord p ( a ) \mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a) as p p varies.
As a prototype of our results, consider the simplest case of Artin’s conjecture, namely that the proportion of primes p p for which 2 2 is a primitive root is Artin’s constant ∏ q ( 1 − 1 / q ⁡ ( q − 1 ) ) \prod_{q}\Bigl(1-{1}/{q(q-1)}\Bigr) .
Consider the deformation ∏ q ( 1 − ( 1 − z ) / q ⁡ ( q − 1 ) ) \prod_{q}\Bigl(1-(1-z)/{q(q-1)}\Bigr) of this product, which admits a power series in z z whose constant term is precisely Artin’s constant; in other words, the constant term measures the density of primes p p for which ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( 2 ) = 1 (p-1)/{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(2)}=1 .
The starting point of our investigation is the observation that the other power series coefficients also have relatively simple interpretations in terms of ord p ( 2 ) \mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(2) : the coefficient of z n z^{n} is the density of those primes p p for which ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( 2 ) (p-1)/{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(2)} has precisely n n distinct prime factors.
Below, we will generalize this observation considerably (to rational a a and to other possible deformations of the product), prove our generalizations on GRH, and obtain weaker versions of our conjectures unconditionally. Throughout this article, p p and q q denote prime numbers, and a a denotes a rational number not equal to − 1 -1 , 0 0 , or 1 1 .
We define ν p ​ ( a ) \nu_{p}(a) to be the unique integer ν \nu such that a = p ν ​ m n a=p^{\nu}\frac{m}{n} where p p divides neither m m nor n n . In other words, if p p divides the numerator of a a (including a a itself if a a is an integer) then ν p ​ ( a ) \nu_{p}(a) is the power to which it divides the numerator, while if p p divides the denominator of a a then − ν p ​ ( a ) -\nu_{p}(a) is the power to which it divides the denominator.
We use the standard notation ω ⁡ ( n ) \omega(n) for the number of distinct prime factors of n n and Ω ⁡ ( n ) \Omega(n) for the number of prime factors of n n counted with multiplicity. 1.1. Statement of results: generating functions We compare p − 1 p-1 with ord p ( a ) \mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a) in three ways. The first way is to count the number of distinct prime factors of their quotient, namely ω ⁡ ( ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) ) \omega\Bigl((p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)\Bigr) , which is the same as the number of distinct prime factors of the index of the subgroup ⟨ a ⟩ \langle a\rangle in ( ℤ / p ​ ℤ ) ∗ ({\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}})^{*} . As this quantity is a nonnegative integer, it makes sense to frame its limiting distribution in terms of a generating function; our first theorem gives an exact formula for that generating function. Theorem 1.4 . Assume GRH. If a ∈ ℚ a\in{\mathbb{Q}} is not a perfect power, then for any z ∈ ℂ z\in{\mathbb{C}} with | z | ≤ 1 |z|\leq 1 , 1 π ⁡ ( x ) ∑ p ≤ x ν p ​ ( a ) = 0 z ω ⁡ ( ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) ) = F a ω / ( z ) ∏ q ( 1 + z − 1 q ⁡ ( q − 1 ) ) + O ( x ​ log ⁡ log ​ x ( log ⁡ x ) 2 ) \frac{1}{\pi(x)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq x\\

F^{\omega/}_{a}(z)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }\mathfrak{d}(a)\not\equiv 1{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $4$)}},\\
1+\displaystyle\prod_{q\mid 2\mathfrak{d}(a)}\frac{z-1}{z+q^{2}-q-1},&\text{if }\mathfrak{d}(a)\equiv 1{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $4$)}}.\end{cases} Remark 1.5 . The condition “ a a is not a perfect power” in the statement of Theorem 1.4 can be relaxed, but the factor in front of the infinite product becomes more complicated in the general case. The same remark applies to Theorems 1.10 and 1.12 below.
The most general versions of these three theorems appear later as Theorems 5.4 , 5.7 , and 5.9 . Remark 1.6 . If we set z = 0 z=0 in Theorem 1.4 , we recover Artin’s conjecture as given in Theorem 1.2 (see Remark 5.5 below for a longer discussion of this reduction). The same remark applies to Theorem 1.10 below. We give concrete examples of these generating functions for three specific values of a a . Remark 1.8 . Note that the case a = 4 a=4 is uninteresting in the original context of Artin’s conjecture, since a perfect square is never a primitive root modulo any odd prime. This fact is reflected in the second generating function in Corollary 1.7 , since the factor 2 ​ z 2z on the right-hand side ensures that there are no terms of the form z 0 z^{0} in the generating function. However, even in this case it is still interesting to examine the full distribution of the statistic ω ⁡ ( ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( 4 ) ) \omega\Bigl((p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(4)\Bigr) , and the generating function accomplishes this goal. A similar remark applies to Corollary 1.11 below. Our second way of comparing p − 1 p-1 with ord p ( a ) \mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a) is the same as the first except that we count the prime factors of ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) (p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a) with multiplicity. The formula becomes more complicated in a way that depends upon the factorization of a a ; the following notation, which we will use throughout this paper, provides us with the needed specificity. Notation 1.9 . Given a ∈ ℚ ∖ { − 1 , 0 , 1 } a\in{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus\{-1,0,1\} , the symbols h , a 0 , b 0 , c 0 h,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0} will always have the following meanings: • We write a = ± a 0 h a=\pm a_{0}^{h} where a 0 a_{0} is positive and not a perfect power, so that h h is the largest integer for which | a | |a| is an h h th power. • We write a 0 = b 0 ​ c 0 2 a_{0}=b_{0}c_{0}^{2} where b 0 = s ⁡ ( a 0 ) b_{0}=s(a_{0}) is squarefree. Our third and final way of comparing p − 1 p-1 with ord p ( a ) \mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a) is to compute the number of distinct primes dividing one but not the other, namely ω ⁡ ( p − 1 ) − ω ⁡ ( ord p ( a ) ) \omega(p-1)-\omega(\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)) . Note that this third statistic is not directly connected to Artin’s conjecture, as ω ⁡ ( p − 1 ) − ω ⁡ ( ord p ( a ) ) \omega(p-1)-\omega(\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)) can equal 0 0 without a a being a primitive root modulo p p (as the example p = 17 p=17 and a = 2 a=2 shows). However the limiting distribution of ω ⁡ ( p − 1 ) − ω ⁡ ( ord p ( a ) ) \omega(p-1)-\omega(\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)) remains interesting in its own right. Theorem 1.12 . Assume GRH. All of these results and their generalizations are proved together in stages. In
Section 2 we axiomatize the combinatorial setup used by Hooley in his proof of Artin’s conjecture, and then apply that generalization to each of our three statistics. The resulting formulas involve degrees of arbitrary cyclotomic-Kummer extensions of the form [ ℚ ( a ℓ , ζ m ​ ℓ ) : ℚ ] \Bigl[{\mathbb{Q}}\Big(\sqrt[\ell]{a},\zeta_{m\ell}\Big):\mathbb{Q}\Bigr] , which we work out in
Section 3.1 . Again following Hooley’s techniques,
in Section 4 we establish adequate bounds for all the error terms that arise during our strategy. Finally, in
Section 5 we convert the main terms in these asymptotic formulas from infinite sums involving the degrees of these extensions (of which equation ( 1.2 ) is an example) into the infinite products that appear in our theorems. 1.2. Statement of results: individual densities A generating function for a nonnegative integer-valued statistic is valuable if we can extract from it information about its coefficients. So for example, D a ω / ( n ) D_{a}^{\omega/}(n) is the density of the set of primes p p for which ω ⁡ ( ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) ) = n \omega((p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a))=n . Assuming GRH, our theorems show that these densities do indeed exist for all a ∉ { − 1 , 0 , 1 } a\notin\{-1,0,1\} .
Moreover, our generating function formulas allow numerical evaluation of the densities D a ω / ( n ) D_{a}^{\omega/}(n) , D a Ω ​ ( n ) D_{a}^{\Omega}(n) , and D a ω − ​ ( n ) D_{a}^{\omega-}(n) to high precision (see Section 6 for details and examples). From their generating functions, we can also employ techniques from complex analysis to deduce decay rates for these densities. The following result is proved in Section 7.1 ): Corollary 1.14 . Assume GRH. For any a ∈ ℚ ∖ { − 1 , 0 , 1 } a\in{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus\{-1,0,1\} : (a) D a Ω ​ ( n ) = R a ​ 4 − n + O a ​ ( 9 − n ) D_{a}^{\Omega}(n)=R_{a}4^{-n}+O_{a}(9^{-n}) , where R a = 3 2 ​ H h Ω ​ ( 4 ) ​ ( 1 + I h Ω ​ ( γ , 4 ) ) ​ ∏ q ≥ 3 ( 1 + 3 ​ q ( q − 1 ) ​ ( q 2 − 4 ) ) R_{a}=\tfrac{3}{2}H_{h}^{\Omega}(4)\Bigl(1+I_{h}^{\Omega}(\gamma,4)\Bigr)\prod_{q\geq 3}\biggl(1+\frac{3q}{(q-1)(q^{2}-4)}\biggr) and H h Ω ​ ( z ) H^{\Omega}_{h}(z) and I h Ω ​ ( γ , z ) I^{\Omega}_{h}(\gamma,z) are as in Definition 5.6 below. (b) Both D a ω / ( n ) ≤ n − ( 2 − o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ n D_{a}^{\omega/}(n)\leq n^{-(2-o(1))n} and D a ω − ​ ( n ) ≤ n − ( 2 − o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ n D_{a}^{\omega-}(n)\leq n^{-(2-o(1))n} (with the implied constants depending on a a ), with each of these upper bounds being attained infinitely often. These densities represent the limiting probability that the given statistics take a certain value if we choose a prime p p “at random”; in other words, for each statistic (and each value of a a ) there is a nonnegative integer-valued random variable whose distribution function is the same as the sequence of densities. Such a random variable has an expectation, which we can interpret as the average value of the statistic when we sample it over randomly chosen primes. We compute exact values and numerical approximations for these expectations in Section 7.2 . All of the above results are conditional on GRH (just as Hooley’s proof of Artin’s conjecture is); however, we are able to obtain some unconditional results. More precisely, we can derive some unconditional inequalities for these densities and for the expectations of the three statistics. The following corollary gives two examples: Corollary 1.15 . Let a a be a rational number that is not a perfect power.
Unconditionally, # { p ≤ x , ν p ( a ) = 0 : ord p ( a ) = p − 1 } \displaystyle\#\bigl\{p\leq x,\,\nu_{p}(a)=0\colon\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)=p-1\bigr\} ≤ ( F a ​ ∏ q ( 1 − 1 q ⁡ ( q − 1 ) ) + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ π ​ ( x ) \displaystyle\leq\biggl(F_{a}\prod_{q}\biggl(1-\frac{1}{q(q-1)}\biggr)+o(1)\biggr)\pi(x) # { p ≤ x , ν p ( a ) = 0 : ω ( ord p ( a ) ) = ω ( p − 1 ) } \displaystyle\#\bigl\{p\leq x,\,\nu_{p}(a)=0\colon\omega(\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a))=\omega(p-1)\bigr\} ≤ ( F a ω − ​ ( 0 ) ​ ∏ q ( 1 − 1 q 2 − 1 ) + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ π ​ ( x ) . \displaystyle\leq\biggl(F_{a}^{\omega-}(0)\prod_{q}\biggl(1-\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\biggr)+o(1)\biggr)\pi(x). in the notation of Theorems 1.2 and 1.12 .
All implied constants may depend on a a . The first statement recovers a result of Vinogradov and Van der Waall from the 1970s, as described in [ 9 ] (just before the start of Section 6.1).
We give generalizations of this corollary in Theorems 8.1 and 8.8 below.
We note that assuming GRH, all of these inequalities become asymptotic formulas. 2. Extending Hooley’s combinatorics In Hooley’s conditional proof of Artin’s conjecture [ 6 ] , he began with a combinatorial decomposition (similar to an inclusion–exclusion sieve) based on the primes dividing p − 1 p-1 that could obstruct a a being a primitive root modulo p p . Our work requires a similar combinatorial setup, with the additional complexity of needing a decomposition involving all powers of primes, rather than just the primes themselves. Moreover, the relationship among the “obstructions” indexed by powers of a given prime is important to the combinatorial setup. For these reasons, in Sections 2.1 and 2.2 we will describe these combinatorial setups in an axiomatic way, with the hope that other authors will find their generality useful. Afterwards, we will apply these setups to our three sets of counting functions in Section 2.3 . Throughout Section 2 , when h ⁡ ( m ) h(m) is a multiplicative function, we let (2.1) g ⁡ ( m ) = ∑ d | m μ ⁡ ( d ) ​ h ​ ( m / d ) and g ∗ ​ ( m ) = ∑ d | m ( d , m / d ) = 1 ( − 1 ) ω ⁡ ( d ) ​ h ​ ( m / d ) g(m)=\sum_{d\mid m}\mu(d)h(m/d)\quad\text{and}\quad g^{*}(m)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid m\\
(d,m/d)=1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\omega(d)}h(m/d) denote its Dirichlet inverse and its unitary inverse, respectively. Also, for any positive real number ξ \xi , we define Q ξ Q_{\xi} to be the least common multiple of all positive integers less than or equal to ξ \xi . Furthermore, throughout Section 2 , let 𝒰 ⊂ ℕ \mathcal{U}\subset{\mathbb{N}} be the “universe” of numbers under consideration.
For every prime power q k q^{k} , let ℱ q k \mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} denote a subset of 𝒰 \mathcal{U} .
For later convenience, we also define ℱ 1 = 𝒰 \mathcal{F}_{1}=\mathcal{U} . We can think of ℱ q k \mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} as being a property that a natural number might or might not have; the letter ℱ \mathcal{F} is chosen to help us think of the numbers in ℱ q k \mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} as having “failed” a test administered by q k q^{k} . We assume that every infinite intersection of various ℱ q k \mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} is empty, so that any given integer n n has only finitely many of the properties ℱ q k \mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} . 2.1. Cumulative properties It might be the case that the collection { ℱ q k } \{\mathcal{F}_{q^{k}}\} has the property that ℱ q k ⊆ ℱ q k − 1 \mathcal{F}_{q^{k}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{q^{k-1}} for all prime powers q k q^{k} , that is, numbers in our universe 𝒰 \mathcal{U} with property ℱ q k \mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} automatically have property ℱ q k − 1 \mathcal{F}_{q^{k-1}} (and thus all properties ℱ q j \mathcal{F}_{q^{j}} with 0 ≤ j ≤ k 0\leq j\leq k , property ℱ 1 \mathcal{F}_{1} being automatic). We call such collections “cumulative”, and for all positive integers ℓ \ell we use the following notation: • We define new sets 𝒜 ℓ = ⋂ q k | ℓ ℱ q k \mathcal{A}_{\ell}=\bigcap_{q^{k}\mid\ell}\mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} (with the interpretation 𝒜 1 = 𝒰 \mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathcal{U} ), so that a number n n has property 𝒜 ℓ \mathcal{A}_{\ell} if and only if it has property ℱ q k \mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} for all prime powers q k | ℓ q^{k}\mid\ell .
Note that such integers have at least those properties (hence the letter 𝒜 \mathcal{A} ) but might have further properties as well, whether from higher powers of existing primes or from new primes. • We also define A ℓ A_{\ell} to be the indicator function of 𝒜 ℓ \mathcal{A}_{\ell} (that is, the function defined by A ℓ ​ ( n ) = 1 A_{\ell}(n)=1 if n ∈ 𝒜 ℓ n\in\mathcal{A}_{\ell} and A ℓ ​ ( n ) = 0 A_{\ell}(n)=0 if n ∉ 𝒜 ℓ n\notin\mathcal{A}_{\ell} ). • Furthermore, we define ℰ ℓ = 𝒜 ℓ ∖ ⋃ q k ∤ ℓ ℱ q k \mathcal{E}_{\ell}=\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\setminus\bigcup_{q^{k}\nmid\ell}\mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} , so that a number in ℰ ℓ \mathcal{E}_{\ell} has exactly the properties ℱ q k \mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} for all prime powers q k | ℓ q^{k}\mid\ell and no other properties (hence the letter ℰ \mathcal{E} ). • Define e ⁡ ( n ) e(n) to be the unique number such that n ∈ ℰ e ⁡ ( n ) n\in\mathcal{E}_{e(n)} , which exists for all n n by the infinite-intersection property and the cumulative property of the ℱ q k \mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} ; note that by cumulativeness, n ∈ 𝒜 ℓ n\in\mathcal{A}_{\ell} if and only if ℓ | e ⁡ ( n ) \ell\mid e(n) . Further, define e ξ ​ ( n ) = gcd ⁡ ( e ⁡ ( n ) , Q ξ ) e_{\xi}(n)=\gcd(e(n),Q_{\xi}) , which we think of as recording which tests up to ξ \xi the number n n fails. • Finally, define N ξ ​ ( x ) = ∑ n ≤ x n ∈ 𝒰 h ⁡ ( e ξ ​ ( n ) ) and P ℓ ​ ( x ) = # ⁡ { n ≤ x : n ∈ 𝒜 ℓ } = ∑ n ≤ x n ∈ 𝒰 A ℓ ​ ( n ) . ∎ 2.2. Disjoint properties On the other hand, it might be the case that the collection { ℱ q k } \{\mathcal{F}_{q^{k}}\} has the property that ℱ q j ∩ ℱ q k = ∅ \mathcal{F}_{q^{j}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{q^{k}}=\emptyset for all primes p p and all distinct positive integers j j and k k , that is, numbers with the property ℱ q j \mathcal{F}_{q^{j}} cannot also have the property ℱ q k \mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} for any positive j ≠ k j\neq k . We call such collections “disjoint”, and for all positive integers ℓ \ell we use the following notation: • We define new sets 𝒜 ℓ ∗ = ⋂ q k | ℓ ℱ q k \mathcal{A}^{*}_{\ell}=\bigcap_{q^{k}\parallel\ell}\mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} (with the interpretation 𝒜 1 ∗ = 𝒰 \mathcal{A}^{*}_{1}=\mathcal{U} ), so that a number n n has property 𝒜 ℓ ∗ \mathcal{A}^{*}_{\ell} if and only if it has property ℱ q k \mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} for all prime powers q k q^{k} exactly dividing ℓ \ell . (Recall that q k | ℓ q^{k}\parallel\ell means that q k | ℓ q^{k}\mid\ell but q k + 1 ∤ ℓ q^{k+1}\nmid\ell .) Note that such integers have at least those properties but might have further properties as well from powers of other primes, although not from other powers of primes already dividing ℓ \ell due to disjointness. • We also define A ℓ ∗ A^{*}_{\ell} to be the indicator function of 𝒜 ℓ ∗ \mathcal{A}^{*}_{\ell} (that is, the function defined by A ℓ ∗ ​ ( n ) = 1 A^{*}_{\ell}(n)=1 if n ∈ 𝒜 ℓ ∗ n\in\mathcal{A}^{*}_{\ell} and A ℓ ∗ ​ ( n ) = 0 A^{*}_{\ell}(n)=0 if n ∉ 𝒜 ℓ ∗ n\notin\mathcal{A}^{*}_{\ell} ). • Furthermore, we define ℰ ℓ ∗ = 𝒜 ℓ ∗ ∖ ⋃ q k ∦ ℓ ℱ q k \mathcal{E}^{*}_{\ell}=\mathcal{A}^{*}_{\ell}\setminus\bigcup_{q^{k}\nparallel\ell}\mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} , so that a number in ℰ ℓ ∗ \mathcal{E}^{*}_{\ell} has exactly the properties ℱ q k \mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} for all prime powers q k | ℓ q^{k}\parallel\ell and no other properties. • Define e ∗ ​ ( n ) e^{*}(n) to be the unique number such that n ∈ ℰ e ∗ ​ ( n ) ∗ n\in\mathcal{E}^{*}_{e^{*}(n)} , which exists for all n n by the infinite-intersection property and the disjoint property of the ℱ q k \mathcal{F}_{q^{k}} ; note that by disjointness, n ∈ ℰ ℓ ∗ n\in\mathcal{E}^{*}_{\ell} if and only if ℓ \ell is a unitary divisor of e ∗ ​ ( n ) e^{*}(n) , that is, if and only if ℓ | e ∗ ​ ( n ) \ell\mid e^{*}(n) and ( ℓ , e ∗ ​ ( n ) / ℓ ) = 1 (\ell,e^{*}(n)/\ell)=1 . ∎ We remark that both formulas in Lemmas 2.1 and 2.2 involve finite sums of finite counting functions (for any given ξ \xi , x x , and y y ), and so no convergence arguments are needed. 2.3. Our three applications In Hooley’s conditional proof of Artin’s conjecture for a a , his method amounts to applying Lemma 2.1 in the case where h ⁡ ( m ) h(m) is the indicator function of m = 1 m=1 , the universe 𝒰 \mathcal{U} is the set of primes not dividing the numerator or denominator of a a (we write ν p ​ ( a ) = 0 \nu_{p}(a)=0 for this condition), and (2.7) ℱ q = { p ∈ 𝒰 : p ≡ 1 (mod q ) , a is a q th power (mod p ) } , while ℱ q k = ∅ for k ≥ 2 . \mathcal{F}_{q}=\{p\in\mathcal{U}\colon p\equiv 1{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $q$)}},\,a\text{ is a $q$th power}{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $p$)}}\},\quad\text{while }\mathcal{F}_{q^{k}}=\emptyset\text{ for }k\geq 2. ∎ 3. The connection to cyclotomic-Kummer extensions In light of the propositions in the previous section, it is important to study the quantity (3.1) P a , ℓ , m ( x ) = # { p ≤ x : ν p ( a ) = 0 , ℓ ∣ ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) , m ℓ ∣ ( p − 1 ) } . P_{a,\ell,m}(x)=\#\Big\{p\leq x\colon\nu_{p}(a)=0,\,\ell\mid(p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a),\ m\ell\mid(p-1)\Big\}. This is the quantity appearing in the main term of Proposition 2.6 ; moreover, the special case m = 1 m=1 is also the quantity appearing in the main terms of Propositions 2.3 and 2.5 ). We immediately rewrite this quantity as (3.2) P a , ℓ , m ( x ) = # { p ≤ x : ν p ( a ) = 0 , p splits completely in ℚ ⁡ ( a ℓ , ζ m ​ ℓ ) ) } . P_{a,\ell,m}(x)=\#\Big\{p\leq x\colon\nu_{p}(a)=0,\,\text{$p$ splits completely in ${\mathbb{Q}}\bigl(\sqrt[\ell]{a},\zeta_{m\ell}\bigr)$})\Big\}. This approach is the one used by Hooley [ 6 ] , although he needed only the special case where ℓ \ell is squarefree and m = 1 m=1 . The fact that these two quantities are identical follows from [ 11 , Proposition 8] ; in that work this fact is stated under the assumption that ℓ \ell is squarefree (since that was the only case required for the application), but the proof therein actually works for arbitrary ℓ \ell . The expression ( 3.2 ) for P a , ℓ , m ​ ( x ) P_{a,\ell,m}(x) can be estimated using the Chebotarev density theorem (indeed, in this particular case of splitting completely, it can be derived directly from the prime ideal theorem). We take exactly this approach in Section 3.3 ; but first we need to understand the degrees (and bound the discriminants) of the cyclotomic-Kummer extensions ℚ ⁡ ( a ℓ , ζ m ​ ℓ ) {\mathbb{Q}}\bigl(\sqrt[\ell]{a},\zeta_{m\ell}\bigr) ; we address these two requirements in Sections 3.1 and 3.2 , respectively. 3.1. Degrees of cyclotomic-Kummer extensions In the case m = 1 m=1 , the degree of the pure Kummer extension ℚ ⁡ ( a ℓ , ζ ℓ ) {\mathbb{Q}}\bigl(\sqrt[\ell]{a},\zeta_{\ell}\bigr) was computed by Wagstaff ( [ 15 , Propostion 4.1] ). For the cyclotomic-Kummer extensions ℚ ⁡ ( a ℓ , ζ m ​ ℓ ) {\mathbb{Q}}\bigl(\sqrt[\ell]{a},\zeta_{m\ell}\bigr) with ℓ \ell squarefree, the degree was computed by the third author [ 11 ] . In this section we carry out the analogous calculation for arbitrary ℓ \ell and m m . To do so, we first need to generalize a result of Schinzel [ 14 , Lemma 4] . Lemma 3.1 . Fix positive integers m m and ℓ {\ell} .
A rational number β \beta is of the form γ ℓ \gamma^{\ell} for some γ ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \gamma\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{m\ell}) if and only if one of the following sets of conditions is satisfied: • ℓ ≡ 1 ​ (mod 2 ) {\ell}\equiv 1{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $2$)}} and β = c ℓ \beta=c^{\ell} for some c ∈ ℚ c\in{\mathbb{Q}} . • ℓ ≡ 0 ​ (mod 2 ) {\ell}\equiv 0{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $2$)}} , β > 0 \beta>0 , and β = c ℓ / 2 \beta=c^{{\ell}/2} for some c ∈ ℚ c\in{\mathbb{Q}} such that c ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \sqrt{c}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{m\ell}) . • ℓ ≡ 0 ​ (mod 2 ) {\ell}\equiv 0{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $2$)}} , β < 0 \beta<0 , 2 | m 2\mid m , and β = − c ℓ / 2 \beta=-c^{{\ell}/2} for some c ∈ ℚ c\in{\mathbb{Q}} such that c ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \sqrt{c}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{m\ell}) . • ℓ ≡ 2 ​ (mod 4 ) {\ell}\equiv 2{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $4$)}} , β < 0 \beta<0 , 2 ∤ m 2\nmid m , and β = c ℓ / 2 \beta=c^{{\ell}/2} for some c ∈ ℚ c\in{\mathbb{Q}} such that c ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \sqrt{c}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{m\ell}) . • ℓ ≡ 4 ​ (mod 8 ) {\ell}\equiv 4{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $8$)}} , β < 0 \beta<0 , 2 ∤ m 2\nmid m , and β = − ( 2 ​ c ) ℓ / 2 \beta=-(2c)^{{\ell}/2} for some c ∈ ℚ c\in{\mathbb{Q}} such that c ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \sqrt{c}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{m\ell}) . Proof. It is easy to check that each set of conditions does imply that β \beta has the required form. For the converse, we suppose that β \beta is indeed of the form γ ℓ \gamma^{\ell} with γ ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \gamma\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{m\ell}) . We write β = sgn ​ ( β ) ​ β 1 t \beta=\textup{sgn}(\beta)\beta_{1}^{t} where β 1 \beta_{1} a positive rational number that is not a perfect power. First suppose that sgn ​ ( β ) = 1 \textup{sgn}(\beta)=1 . Then β 1 t / ℓ ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \beta_{1}^{t/{\ell}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{m\ell}) and thus ℚ ⁡ ( β 1 t / ℓ ) {\mathbb{Q}}(\beta_{1}^{t/{\ell}}) is normal over ℚ {\mathbb{Q}} . Furthermore, the polynomial X ℓ / ( ℓ , t ) − β 1 t / ( ℓ , t ) X^{{\ell}/({\ell},t)}-\beta_{1}^{t/({\ell},t)} is irreducible over ℚ {\mathbb{Q}} and has β 1 t / ℓ \beta_{1}^{t/{\ell}} as one of its roots, and therefore all its ℓ / ( ℓ , t ) {\ell}/({\ell},t) roots (which are equally spaced around a circle in ℂ {\mathbb{C}} ) must lie in ℚ ⁡ ( β 1 t / ℓ ) {\mathbb{Q}}(\beta_{1}^{t/{\ell}}) . But ℚ ⁡ ( β 1 t / ℓ ) {\mathbb{Q}}(\beta_{1}^{t/{\ell}}) is a real subfield of ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) {\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{m\ell}) , which forces X ℓ / ( ℓ , t ) − β 1 t / ( ℓ , t ) X^{{\ell}/({\ell},t)}-\beta_{1}^{t/({\ell},t)} to have at most two roots; it follows that ℓ / ( ℓ , t ) ≤ 2 {\ell}/({\ell},t)\leq 2 , which implies that ℓ | 2 ​ t {\ell}\mid 2t . • If ℓ ≡ 1 ​ (mod 2 ) {\ell}\equiv 1{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $2$)}} , then ℓ | t {\ell}\mid t and so c = β 1 t / ℓ ∈ ℚ c=\beta_{1}^{t/{\ell}}\in{\mathbb{Q}} satisfies β = c ℓ \beta=c^{\ell} . • If ℓ ≡ 0 ​ (mod 2 ) {\ell}\equiv 0{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $2$)}} , then c = β 1 2 ​ t / ℓ ∈ ℚ c=\beta_{1}^{2t/{\ell}}\in{\mathbb{Q}} satisfies β = c ℓ / 2 \beta=c^{{\ell}/2} and c = β 1 t / ℓ ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \sqrt{c}=\beta_{1}^{t/{\ell}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{m\ell}) . Now suppose that sgn ​ ( β ) = − 1 \textup{sgn}(\beta)=-1 . Then ζ 2 ​ ℓ ​ β 1 t / ℓ ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \zeta_{2{\ell}}\beta_{1}^{t/{\ell}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{m\ell}) where ζ 2 ​ ℓ \zeta_{2{\ell}} is a primitive 2 ​ ℓ 2{\ell} th root of unity. • If ℓ ≡ 1 ​ (mod 2 ) {\ell}\equiv 1{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $2$)}} , then ζ 2 ​ ℓ ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ ℓ ) ⊂ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \zeta_{2{\ell}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{{\ell}})\subset{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{{m\ell}}) , so β 1 t / ℓ ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \beta_{1}^{t/{\ell}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{{m\ell}}) and hence (as in the first case) c = β 1 t / ℓ ∈ ℚ c=\beta_{1}^{t/{\ell}}\in{\mathbb{Q}} satisfies β = c ℓ \beta=c^{\ell} . • If ℓ ≡ 0 ​ (mod 2 ) {\ell}\equiv 0{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $2$)}} and 2 | m 2\mid m , then ζ 2 ​ ℓ ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \zeta_{2{\ell}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{{m\ell}}) , so β 1 t / ℓ ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \beta_{1}^{t/{\ell}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{{m\ell}}) and hence (as in the first case) c = β 1 2 ​ t / ℓ ∈ ℚ c=\beta_{1}^{2t/{\ell}}\in{\mathbb{Q}} satisfies β = c ℓ / 2 \beta=c^{{\ell}/2} and c = β 1 t / ℓ ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \sqrt{c}=\beta_{1}^{t/{\ell}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{m\ell}) . The remaining case is where sgn ​ ( β ) = − 1 \textup{sgn}(\beta)=-1 and ℓ ≡ 0 ​ (mod 2 ) {\ell}\equiv 0{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $2$)}} but 2 ∤ m 2\nmid m .
Then ζ 2 ​ ℓ ∉ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \zeta_{2{\ell}}\notin{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{{m\ell}}) so β 1 t / ℓ ∉ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \beta_{1}^{t/{\ell}}\notin{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{{m\ell}}) . It follows that β 1 ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ 2 ​ m ​ ℓ ) \sqrt{\beta_{1}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{2{m\ell}}) and β 1 ∉ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \sqrt{\beta_{1}}\notin{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{{m\ell}}) . Writing s ⁡ ( β 1 ) s(\beta_{1}) for the squarefree kernel of β 1 \beta_{1} , we deduce from [ 14 , Lemma 3] that s ⁡ ( β 1 ) | m ​ ℓ s(\beta_{1})\mid{m\ell} and that either m ​ ℓ ≡ 2 ​ (mod 4 ) {m\ell}\equiv 2{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $4$)}} and s ⁡ ( β 1 ) ≡ 3 ​ (mod 4 ) s(\beta_{1})\equiv 3{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $4$)}} , or m ​ ℓ ≡ 4 ​ (mod 8 ) {m\ell}\equiv 4{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $8$)}} and 2 | s ⁡ ( β 1 ) 2\mid s(\beta_{1}) . By the same lemma: • when m ​ ℓ ≡ 2 ​ (mod 4 ) {m\ell}\equiv 2{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $4$)}} and s ⁡ ( β 1 ) ≡ 3 ​ (mod 4 ) s(\beta_{1})\equiv 3{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $4$)}} , we also have ℓ ≡ 2 ​ (mod 4 ) {\ell}\equiv 2{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $4$)}} , and c = − β 1 2 ​ t / ℓ ∈ ℚ c=-\beta_{1}^{2t/{\ell}}\in{\mathbb{Q}} satisfies β = c ℓ / 2 \beta=c^{{\ell}/2} and c ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \sqrt{c}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{m\ell}) ; • when m ​ ℓ ≡ 4 ​ (mod 8 ) {m\ell}\equiv 4{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $8$)}} and 2 | s ⁡ ( β 1 ) 2\mid s(\beta_{1}) , we also have ℓ ≡ 4 ​ (mod 8 ) {\ell}\equiv 4{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $8$)}} , and c = − ( β 1 ) 2 ​ t / ℓ / 2 ∈ ℚ c=-(\beta_{1})^{2t/{\ell}}/2\in{\mathbb{Q}} satisfies β = − ( 2 ​ c ) ℓ / 2 \beta=-(2c)^{{\ell}/2} and c ∈ ℚ ⁡ ( ζ m ​ ℓ ) \sqrt{c}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{m\ell}) .
∎ We are now able to generalize Wagstaff’s argument. Recall the notation from Definition 1.1 and Notation 1.9 . Proposition 3.2 . Let a ∈ ℚ ∖ { − 1 , 0 , 1 } a\in{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus\{-1,0,1\} . ∎ 3.2. ∎ 3.3. Asymptotic behavior of P a , ℓ , m ​ ( x ) P_{a,\ell,m}(x) We now return to the estimation of P a , ℓ , m ​ ( x ) P_{a,\ell,m}(x) from equation ( 3.2 ). Theorem 3.4 . Assume GRH. Let a ∈ ℚ ∖ { − 1 , 0 , 1 } a\in{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus\{-1,0,1\} , and let ℓ \ell and m m be positive integers. Then P a , ℓ , m ( x ) = π ⁡ ( x ) [ ℚ ( a ℓ , ζ m ​ ℓ ) : ℚ ] + O a ( x log ( x ℓ ) ) = ε a ​ ( m ​ ℓ , ℓ ) ⋅ ( ℓ , h ) ℓ ​ φ ​ ( m ​ ℓ ) π ( x ) + O a ( x log ( x ℓ ) ) , P_{a,\ell,m}(x)=\frac{\pi(x)}{\Bigl[{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[\ell]{a},\zeta_{m\ell}):{\mathbb{Q}}\Bigr]}+{\mathit{O}}_{a}\Bigl(\sqrt{x}\log(x\ell)\Bigr)=\frac{\varepsilon_{a}(m\ell,\ell)\cdot(\ell,h)}{\ell\varphi(m\ell)}\pi(x)+{\mathit{O}}_{a}\Bigl(\sqrt{x}\log(x\ell)\Bigr), where ε a ​ ( m ​ ℓ , ℓ ) \varepsilon_{a}(m\ell,\ell) and h h were defined in Proposition 3.2 and Notation 1.9 , respectively. Proof. Lang [ 8 ] established, assuming GRH, the following effective prime ideal theorem for a number field K K (a special case of a Chebotarev density theorem with effective error term): uniformly in K K , (3.4) π K ( x ) = π ( x ) + O ( x log ( x [ K : ℚ ] | Δ K | ) ) , \pi_{K}(x)=\pi(x)+{\mathit{O}}\left(\sqrt{x}\log(x^{[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]}|\Delta_{K}|)\right), where π K ​ ( x ) = # ⁡ { 𝔭 : N ⁡ ( 𝔭 ) ≤ x } \pi_{K}(x)=\#\{\mathfrak{p}\colon N(\mathfrak{p})\leq x\} is the prime ideal counting function for K K (and π ​ ( x ) = π ℚ ​ ( x ) \pi(x)=\pi_{\mathbb{Q}}(x) is the ordinary prime counting function) and Δ K \Delta_{K} is the discriminant of K K . If we take K = ℚ ⁡ ( a ℓ , ζ m ​ ℓ ) K={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[\ell]{a},\zeta_{m\ell}) , then Proposition 3.3 implies that (3.5) π ℚ ⁡ ( a ℓ , ζ m ​ ℓ ) ( x ) = π ( x ) + O ( [ ℚ ( a ℓ , ζ m ​ ℓ ) : ℚ ] x log ( x ℓ ) ) . \pi_{{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[\ell]{a},\zeta_{m\ell})}(x)=\pi(x)+{\mathit{O}}\Bigl(\Bigl[{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[\ell]{a},\zeta_{m\ell}):{\mathbb{Q}}\Bigr]\sqrt{x}\log(x\ell)\Bigr). Finally, P a , ℓ , m ​ ( x ) P_{a,\ell,m}(x) is almost exactly π ℚ ⁡ ( a ℓ , ζ m ​ ℓ ) ( x ) / [ ℚ ( a ℓ , ζ m ​ ℓ ) : ℚ ] \pi_{{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[\ell]{a},\zeta_{m\ell})}(x)/\Bigl[{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[\ell]{a},\zeta_{m\ell}):{\mathbb{Q}}\Bigr] ; the precise relationship, with a suitably bounded error term that is majorized by the error term above, works here exactly the same way as in Hooley’s work [ 6 , Section 4, equation (17)] . We thus deduce that P a , ℓ , m ( x ) = π ⁡ ( x ) [ ℚ ( a ℓ , ζ m ​ ℓ ) : ℚ ] + O a ( x log ( x ℓ ) ) , P_{a,\ell,m}(x)=\frac{\pi(x)}{\Bigl[{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[\ell]{a},\zeta_{m\ell}):{\mathbb{Q}}\Bigr]}+{\mathit{O}}_{a}\Bigl(\sqrt{x}\log(x\ell)\Bigr), and the second equality follows from Proposition 3.2 .
∎ 4. Dealing with error terms We first deal with the error terms in Propositions 2.3 , 2.5 , and 2.6 ; we may concentrate on the error term in Proposition 2.5 , as it is an upper bound for the error terms in the other two propositions. Our goal is to lower ξ \xi enough so that summing the error term of Theorem 3.4 is admissible. In order to control this error term, we use the same techniques as used by Hooley [ 6 ] . First we will see how low we can get ξ \xi without using GRH. Using a combinatorial argument and the Brun–Titchmarsh theorem we obtain the following proposition. Proposition 4.1 . Let a ∈ ℚ ∖ { − 1 , 0 , 1 } a\in{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus\{-1,0,1\} .
For all B ≥ 0 B\geq 0 , ∑ x ( log ⁡ x ) B < q k ≤ x # { p ≤ x : ν p ( a ) = 0 , q k ∣ ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) } ≪ a , B x ​ log ⁡ log ​ x log 2 ⁡ x . \sum_{\frac{\sqrt{x}}{(\log x)^{B}}<q^{k}\leq x}\#\{p\leq x\colon\nu_{p}(a)=0,\,q^{k}\mid(p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)\}\ll_{a,B}\frac{x\log\log x}{\log^{2}x}. Proof. For the largest prime powers, we see by writing p − 1 = q k ​ m p-1=q^{k}m that ∑ x ​ log ⁡ x < q k ≤ x \displaystyle\sum_{\sqrt{x}\log x<q^{k}\leq x} # { p ≤ x : ν p ( a ) = 0 , q k ∣ ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) } \displaystyle\#\{p\leq x\colon\nu_{p}(a)=0,\,q^{k}\mid(p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)\} = ∑ x ​ log ⁡ x < q k ≤ x # { p ≤ x : ν p ( a ) = 0 , q k ∣ ( p − 1 ) , ord p ( a ) ∣ ( p − 1 ) / q k } \displaystyle=\sum_{\sqrt{x}\log x<q^{k}\leq x}\#\{p\leq x\colon\nu_{p}(a)=0,\,q^{k}\mid(p-1),\,\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)\mid(p-1)/q^{k}\} = ∑ x ​ log ⁡ x < q k ≤ x # { p ≤ x : ν p ( a ) = 0 , q k ∣ ( p − 1 ) , a ( p − 1 ) / q k ≡ 1 (mod p ) } \displaystyle=\sum_{\sqrt{x}\log x<q^{k}\leq x}\#\{p\leq x\colon\nu_{p}(a)=0,\,q^{k}\mid(p-1),\,a^{(p-1)/q^{k}}\equiv 1{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $p$)}}\} ≤ ∑ m < x / log ⁡ x # { p ≤ x : ν p ( a ) = 0 , a m ≡ 1 (mod p ) } \displaystyle\leq\sum_{m<\sqrt{x}/\log x}\#\{p\leq x\colon\nu_{p}(a)=0,\,a^{m}\equiv 1{\text{\rm\ (mod\penalty\ $p$)}}\} ≤ Ω ( ∏ m < x / log ⁡ x ( a m − 1 ) ) ≤ 1 log ⁡ 2 log ( ∏ m ≤ x / log ⁡ x ( a m − 1 ) ) < log ⁡ a log ⁡ 2 ∑ m < x / log ⁡ x m ≪ a x log 2 ⁡ x . \displaystyle\leq\Omega\bigg(\prod_{m<\sqrt{x}/\log x}(a^{m}-1)\bigg)\leq\frac{1}{\log 2}\log\bigg(\prod_{m\leq\sqrt{x}/\log x}(a^{m}-1)\bigg)<\frac{\log a}{\log 2}\sum_{m<\sqrt{x}/\log x}m\ll_{a}\frac{x}{\log^{2}x}. This is entirely correct if a a is a positive integer; if a = n / d a=n/d , then we should replace a m − 1 a^{m}-1 with n m − d m n^{m}-d^{m} , but this only affects the implicit constant in the final bound. ∎ Assuming GRH we can get ξ \xi even lower (exactly as in [ 6 ] ). Proposition 4.2 . Assume GRH. Let a ∈ ℚ ∖ { − 1 , 0 , 1 } a\in{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus\{-1,0,1\} and B ≥ 2 B\geq 2 . For all ξ ≪ ( log ⁡ x ) B − 1 \xi\ll(\log x)^{B-1} , ∑ ξ ≤ q k < x ( log ⁡ x ) B # ( p ≤ x : ν p ( a ) = 0 , q k ∣ ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) ) ≪ B x ξ ​ log ⁡ x . \sum\limits_{\xi\leq q^{k}<\frac{\sqrt{x}}{\left(\log x\right)^{B}}}\#\left(p\leq x\colon\nu_{p}(a)=0,\,q^{k}\mid(p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)\right)\ll_{B}\frac{x}{\xi\log x}. Proof. We apply Theorem 3.4 with ℓ = q k \ell=q^{k} and m = 1 m=1 to obtain ∑ ξ ≤ q k < x ( log ⁡ x ) B # ( p ≤ x : ν p ( a ) = 0 , q k ∣ ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) ) \displaystyle\sum_{\xi\leq q^{k}<\frac{\sqrt{x}}{\left(\log x\right)^{B}}}\#\left(p\leq x\colon\nu_{p}(a)=0,\,q^{k}\mid(p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)\right) = ∑ ξ ≤ q k < x ( log ⁡ x ) B ( π ⁡ ( x ) q 2 ​ k − 1 ​ ( q − 1 ) + O ⁡ ( x ​ log ⁡ x ) ) \displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\leq q^{k}<\frac{\sqrt{x}}{\left(\log x\right)^{B}}}\left(\frac{\pi(x)}{q^{2k-1}(q-1)}+{\mathit{O}}\bigl(\sqrt{x}\log x\bigr)\right) ≪ x log ⁡ x ​ ∑ ξ ≤ q k < x ( log ⁡ x ) A 1 q 2 ​ k + x ( log ⁡ x ) B \displaystyle\ll\frac{x}{\log x}\sum\limits_{\xi\leq q^{k}<\frac{\sqrt{x}}{\left(\log x\right)^{A}}}\frac{1}{q^{2k}}+\frac{x}{(\log x)^{B}} < x log ⁡ x ​ ∑ n > ξ 1 n 2 + x ( log ⁡ x ) B ≪ x ξ ​ log ⁡ x + x ( log ⁡ x ) B , \displaystyle<\frac{x}{\log x}\sum\limits_{n>\xi}\frac{1}{n^{2}}+\frac{x}{(\log x)^{B}}\ll\frac{x}{\xi\log x}+\frac{x}{(\log x)^{B}}, and the first error term dominates by the assumption on ξ \xi .
∎ When we incorporate Theorem 3.4 into Propositions 2.3 , 2.5 , and 2.6 , a new error term will appear from summing the error term from Theorem 3.4 over divisors of Q ξ Q_{\xi} , which we recall is the least common multiple of all the integers up to ξ \xi . The following proposition bounds these errors. Proposition 4.3 . Let x ≥ 3 x\geq 3 and A > 0 A>0 be real numbers. Uniformly for ξ ≤ 1 2 ​ log ⁡ x \xi\leq\frac{1}{2}\log x and | z | ≤ A |z|\leq A , ∑ ℓ | Q ξ μ 2 ​ ( ℓ ) ​ | z − 1 | ω ⁡ ( ℓ ) ​ x ​ log ⁡ ( x ​ ℓ ) \displaystyle\sum\limits_{\ell\mid Q_{\xi}}\mu^{2}(\ell)|z-1|^{\omega(\ell)}\sqrt{x}\log(x\ell) ≤ ( x ​ log ⁡ x ) ​ ( 2 + A ) ξ \displaystyle\leq\bigl(\sqrt{x}\log x\bigr)(2+A)^{\xi} ∑ ℓ | Q ξ | z | Ω ⁡ ( ℓ ) ​ | 1 − z − 1 | ω ⁡ ( ℓ ) ​ x ​ log ⁡ ( x ​ ℓ ) \displaystyle\sum\limits_{\ell\mid Q_{\xi}}|z|^{\Omega(\ell)}|1-z^{-1}|^{\omega(\ell)}\sqrt{x}\log(x\ell) ≤ ( x ​ log ⁡ x ) ​ ( 2 + A ) 4 ​ ξ \displaystyle\leq\bigl(\sqrt{x}\log x\bigr)(2+A)^{4\xi} ∑ ℓ | Q ξ | z − 1 | ω ⁡ ( ℓ ) ​ ∑ m | ℓ μ 2 ​ ( m ) ​ x ​ log ⁡ ( x ​ m ​ ℓ ) \displaystyle\sum\limits_{\ell\mid Q_{\xi}}|z-1|^{\omega(\ell)}\sum\limits_{m\mid\ell}\mu^{2}(m)\sqrt{x}\log(xm\ell) ≤ ( x ​ log ⁡ x ) ​ ( 3 + 2 ​ A ) ξ . \displaystyle\leq\bigl(\sqrt{x}\log x\bigr)(3+2A)^{\xi}. Proof. We first note the Chebyshev-style inequalities ψ ⁡ ( t ) = ∑ n ≤ t Λ ⁡ ( n ) < 1.04 ​ t \psi(t)=\sum_{n\leq t}\Lambda(n)<1.04t [ 12 , Theorem 12] and π ⁡ ( t ) < 1.26 ​ t / log ⁡ t \pi(t)<1.26t/\log t [ 12 , Corollary 1] , both valid for t > 1 t>1 . In particular, Q ξ = e ψ ⁡ ( ξ ) < e 1.04 ⋅ 1 2 ​ log ⁡ x = x 0.52 Q_{\xi}=e^{\psi(\xi)}<e^{1.04\cdot\frac{1}{2}\log x}=x^{0.52} , and thus log ⁡ ( x ​ ℓ ) ≪ log ⁡ x \log(x\ell)\ll\log x and log ⁡ ( x ​ m ​ ℓ ) ≪ log ⁡ x \log(xm\ell)\ll\log x in these sums. It remains to estimate the sums over ℓ \ell . For the first estimate, ∑ ℓ | Q ξ μ 2 ​ ( ℓ ) ​ | z − 1 | ω ⁡ ( ℓ ) = ∏ p | Q ℓ ( 1 + | z − 1 | ) ≤ ∏ p ≤ ξ ( 2 + A ) ≤ ( 2 + A ) ξ . \displaystyle\sum_{\ell\mid Q_{\xi}}\mu^{2}(\ell)|z-1|^{\omega(\ell)}=\prod_{p\mid Q_{\ell}}(1+|z-1|)\leq\prod_{p\leq\xi}(2+A)\leq(2+A)^{\xi}. Finally, for the third estimate, ∑ ℓ | Q ξ | z − 1 | ω ⁡ ( ℓ ) ​ ∑ m | ℓ μ 2 ​ ( m ) \displaystyle\sum_{\ell\mid Q_{\xi}}|z-1|^{\omega(\ell)}\sum\limits_{m\mid\ell}\mu^{2}(m) = ∑ ℓ | Q ξ ( 2 ​ | z − 1 | ) ω ⁡ ( ℓ ) = ∏ p | Q ℓ ( 1 + 2 ​ | z − 1 | ) ≤ ∏ p ≤ ξ ( 3 + 2 ​ A ) ≤ ( 3 + 2 ​ A ) ξ . ∎ \displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\mid Q_{\xi}}(2|z-1|)^{\omega(\ell)}=\prod_{p\mid Q_{\ell}}(1+2|z-1|)\leq\prod_{p\leq\xi}(3+2A)\leq(3+2A)^{\xi}.\qed Finally we are left with the sum over divisors of Q ξ Q_{\xi} of the main term in Theorem 3.4 . But in order to be able to evaluate our different coefficients we would like the sum to be over all integers. It is easy to see that all integers smaller than ξ \xi divide Q ξ Q_{\xi} ; therefore by replacing our sum over divisors of ξ \xi by a sum over all integers, we are introducing an error which is at most the sum over all integers bigger than ξ \xi . First a lemma, letting σ ⁡ ( k ) \sigma(k) be the sum of the positive divisors of k k . Lemma 4.4 . For ξ ≥ 2 \xi\geq 2 , we have ∑ k > ξ 2 ω ⁡ ( k ) ​ σ ​ ( k ) k 2 ​ φ ​ ( k ) ≪ log ⁡ ξ ξ \displaystyle\sum_{k>\xi}\frac{2^{\omega(k)}\sigma(k)}{k^{2}\varphi(k)}\ll\frac{\log\xi}{\xi} . Proof. Define the nonnegative multiplicative function f ⁡ ( k ) = 2 ω ⁡ ( k ) ​ σ ​ ( k ) / φ ⁡ ( k ) f(k)=2^{\omega(k)}\sigma(k)/\varphi(k) , and set F ⁡ ( t ) = ∑ k ≤ t f ⁡ ( k ) F(t)=\sum_{k\leq t}f(k) . We note that f ⁡ ( p ) = 2 ​ ( p + 1 ) / ( p − 1 ) = 2 + O ⁡ ( 1 p ) f(p)=2(p+1)/(p-1)=2+O(\frac{1}{p}) and thus ∑ p ≤ t f ⁡ ( p ) = 2 ​ π ​ ( t ) + O ⁡ ( log ⁡ log ⁡ t ) \sum_{p\leq t}f(p)=2\pi(t)+O(\log\log t) . Furthermore, f ⁡ ( p r ) = 2 ​ ( p r + 1 − 1 ) / p r − 1 ​ ( p − 1 ) 2 < 2 ​ p 2 / ( p − 1 ) 2 ≤ 8 f(p^{r})=2(p^{r+1}-1)/p^{r-1}(p-1)^{2}<2p^{2}/(p-1)^{2}\leq 8 fot all prime powers p r p^{r} . It follows from the Wirsing–Odoni method (see for example [ 5 , Proposition 4] ) that F ⁡ ( t ) ∼ C f ​ t ​ log ⁡ t F(t)\sim C_{f}t\log t for some positive constant C f C_{f} . Summing by parts, we deduce that ∑ k > ξ 2 ω ​ ( k ) ​ σ ​ ( k ) k 2 ​ φ ​ ( k ) \displaystyle\sum_{k>\xi}\frac{2^{\omega}(k)\sigma(k)}{k^{2}\varphi(k)} = ∫ ξ ∞ d ​ F ​ ( t ) t 2 = F ⁡ ( ξ ) ξ 2 + 2 ​ ∫ ξ ∞ F ⁡ ( t ) t 3 ​ 𝑑 t ≪ ξ ​ log ⁡ ξ ξ 2 + ∫ ξ ∞ t ​ log ⁡ t t 3 ​ 𝑑 t ≪ log ⁡ ξ ξ \displaystyle=\int_{\xi}^{\infty}\frac{dF(t)}{t^{2}}=\frac{F(\xi)}{\xi^{2}}+2\int_{\xi}^{\infty}\frac{F(t)}{t^{3}}\,dt\ll\frac{\xi\log\xi}{\xi^{2}}+\int_{\xi}^{\infty}\frac{t\log t}{t^{3}}\,dt\ll\frac{\log\xi}{\xi} as claimed.

∎ Proposition 4.6 . Let a ∈ ℚ ∖ { − 1 , 0 , 1 } a\in{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus\{-1,0,1\} and x ≥ 3 x\geq 3 . Uniformly for | z | ≤ 1 |z|\leq 1 , ∑ p ≤ x ν p ​ ( a ) = 0 z ω ⁡ ( ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) ) \displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq x\\

The proofs of the first and last claims are exactly the same, using the other parts of the propositions and theorems referenced above.
∎ 5. Main terms and the general theorems We are now ready to state and prove our most general results, which are Theorems 5.4 , 5.7 , and 5.9 below. It is straightforward to check that Theorems 1.4 , 1.10 , and 1.12 , respectively, are the special cases of these three general results where h = 1 h=1 . The conclusion of Proposition 4.6 already reflects all the work we have done to bound the error terms in the desired asymptotic formulas, and so it remains only to make the main terms more explicit. If the field extension degree [ ℚ ( a ℓ , ζ m ​ ℓ ) : ℚ ] [{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[\ell]{a},\zeta_{m\ell}):{\mathbb{Q}}] were always equal to ℓ ​ ϕ ​ ( m ​ ℓ ) \ell\phi(m\ell) , then expressing each main term as an Euler product would take only a few lines (and there would be no need for correction factors depending on a a in the statements of the theorems). However, Proposition 3.2 shows that the truth is more complicated, most notably because of the ε a ​ ( ℓ , m ​ ℓ ) \varepsilon_{a}(\ell,m\ell) term; the general strategy we adopt to handle these terms appears in equation ( 5.7 ) below. In the beginning of this section, we fully state all three general results (which are complicated enough to require several auxiliary pieces of notation). The proofs of Theorems 5.4 , 5.7 , and 5.9 will be given afterwards in Sections 5.1 , 5.2 , and 5.3 , respectively. For the purposes of comparison, we first give the analogous general statement for Artin’s primitive root conjecture, as established by Hooley [ 6 ] . ∎ 6. Numerical values Theorems 5.4 , 5.7 , and 5.9 establish the densities of the sets of primes for which ord p ( a ) \mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a) has a particular relationship with p − 1 p-1 .
For example, D a ω / ( n ) D_{a}^{\omega/}(n) is the density of the set of primes p p with ω ⁡ ( ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) ) = n \omega((p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a))=n ; in light of
equations ( 1.3 ) and ( 1.4 ), we can restate
Theorem 5.4 as ∑ n = 0 ∞ D a ω / ( n ) z n = F a ω / ( z ) H a ω / ( z ) ∏ q ( 1 + z − 1 q ⁡ ( q − 1 ) ) . \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}D_{a}^{\omega/}(n)z^{n}=F^{\omega/}_{a}(z)H^{\omega/}_{a}(z)\prod_{q}\biggl(1+\frac{z-1}{q(q-1)}\biggr). In particular, this identity provides us with a way to extract information about the individual densities D a ω / ( n ) D_{a}^{\omega/}(n) from the formula for their generating function on the right-hand side.
The first two factors are simply rational functions of z z that depend on a a , while the third factor is an infinite product that takes some attention to understand. We therefore define “uncorrected” coefficients in Notation 6.2 below so that, for example, ∑ n = 0 ∞ D ω / ( n ) z n = ∏ q ( 1 + z − 1 q ⁡ ( q − 1 ) ) \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}D^{\omega/}(n)z^{n}=\prod_{q}\biggl(1+\frac{z-1}{q(q-1)}\biggr) and concentrate on them initially. In Section 6.1 we provide explicit recursive formulas for these uncorrected coefficients. In Section 6.2 we use these recursive formulas to calculate several examples of numerical values, both for the uncorrected coefficients and the true coefficients such as D a ω / ( n ) D_{a}^{\omega/}(n) . In
Section 6.3 we record a non-recursive formula for the uncorrected coefficients, in the hope that readers might find it useful to adapt to other situations. 6.1. Derivatives of infinite products and recursive formulas for their coefficients This approach we take in this section is based on observations of Marcus Lai (private communication), and a version of it appears in [ 4 ] as well. Proposition 6.1 . Let P ⁡ ( z ) P(z) be any function with Maclaurin series P ⁡ ( z ) = ∑ n = 0 ∞ C ⁡ ( n ) ​ z n , P(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}C(n)z^{n}, so that C ⁡ ( n ) = P ( n ) ​ ( 0 ) / n ! C(n)=P^{(n)}(0)/n! for every n ≥ 0 n\geq 0 . Define S ⁡ ( 0 , z ) = log ⁡ P ⁡ ( z ) and S ⁡ ( n , z ) = d n d ​ z n ​ S ​ ( 0 , z ) S(0,z)=\log P(z)\quad\text{and}\quad S(n,z)=\frac{d^{n}}{dz^{n}}S(0,z) for all n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 ; and define S ⁡ ( n ) = S ⁡ ( n , 0 ) S(n)=S(n,0) and S ~ ​ ( n ) = S ⁡ ( n ) / ( n − 1 ) ! \tilde{S}(n)=S(n)/(n-1)! . Then for any n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , P ( n ) ​ ( z ) \displaystyle P^{(n)}(z) = ∑ k = 0 n − 1 ( n − 1 k ) ​ P ( k ) ​ ( z ) ​ S ​ ( n − k , z ) . \displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{n-1}{k}P^{(k)}(z)S(n-k,z). In particular, for n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , C ⁡ ( n ) = 1 n ​ ∑ k = 0 n − 1 C ⁡ ( k ) ​ S ~ ​ ( n − k ) . C(n)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}C(k)\tilde{S}(n-k). Proof. We first verify that P ′ ​ ( z ) = P ⁡ ( z ) ​ P ′ ​ ( z ) P ⁡ ( z ) = P ⁡ ( z ) ​ d d ​ z ​ log ⁡ P ⁡ ( z ) = P ⁡ ( z ) ​ d d ​ z ​ S ​ ( 0 , z ) = P ⁡ ( z ) ​ S ​ ( 1 , z ) , P^{\prime}(z)=P(z)\frac{P^{\prime}(z)}{P(z)}=P(z)\frac{d}{dz}\log P(z)=P(z)\frac{d}{dz}S(0,z)=P(z)S(1,z), which is the case n = 1 n=1 of the first identity. The general case of the first identity now follows from using the product rule n − 1 n-1 times in a row on this initial identity P ′ ​ ( z ) = P ⁡ ( z ) ​ S ​ ( 1 , z ) P^{\prime}(z)=P(z)S(1,z) . The second identity follows by plugging in z = 0 z=0 to the first and using the definition C ⁡ ( n ) = 1 n ! ​ P ( n ) ​ ( 0 ) C(n)=\frac{1}{n!}P^{(n)}(0) .
∎ We now apply this result to the (uncorrected) infinite products appearing in Theorems 5.4 , 5.7 , and 5.9 . We caution the reader not to conflate the functions we now define, such as f ω / ( q , z ) f_{\omega/}(q,z) , with earlier similarly named functions such as f ω / ( z ) f^{\omega/}(z) from Definition 5.3 . Notation 6.2 . Define f Ω ( q , z ) = q 3 − q 2 − q + z ( q − 1 ) ​ ( q 2 − z ) and f ω / ( q , z ) = ( 1 + z − 1 q ⁡ ( q − 1 ) ) and f ω − ( q , z ) = ( 1 + z − 1 q 2 − 1 ) . f_{\Omega}(q,z)=\frac{q^{3}-q^{2}-q+z}{(q-1)(q^{2}-z)}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{\omega/}(q,z)=\bigg(1+\frac{z-1}{q(q-1)}\bigg)\quad\text{and}\quad f_{\omega-}(q,z)=\bigg(1+\frac{z-1}{q^{2}-1}\bigg). Then the infinite products appearing in Theorems 5.4 , 5.7 , and 5.9 are P Ω ( z ) = ∏ q f Ω ( q , z ) and P ω / ( z ) = ∏ q f ω / ( q , z ) and P ω − ( z ) = ∏ q f ω − ( q , z ) . P_{\Omega}(z)=\prod_{q}f_{\Omega}(q,z)\quad\text{and}\quad P_{\omega/}(z)=\prod_{q}f_{\omega/}(q,z)\quad\text{and}\quad P_{\omega-}(z)=\prod_{q}f_{\omega-}(q,z). We are interested in the coefficients of their Maclaurin series, which we name by P Ω ( z ) = ∑ n = 0 ∞ D Ω ( n ) z n and P ω / ( z ) = ∑ n = 0 ∞ D ω / ( n ) z n and P ω − ( z ) = ∑ n = 0 ∞ D ω − ( n ) z n , P_{\Omega}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}D^{\Omega}(n)z^{n}\quad\text{and}\quad P_{\omega/}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}D^{\omega/}(n)z^{n}\quad\text{and}\quad P_{\omega-}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}D^{\omega-}(n)z^{n}, so that D Ω ( n ) = 1 n ! P Ω ( n ) ( z ) and D ω / ( n ) = 1 n ! P ω / ( n ) ( z ) and D ω − ( n ) = 1 n ! P ω − ( n ) ( z ) . D^{\Omega}(n)=\frac{1}{n!}P^{(n)}_{\Omega}(z)\quad\text{and}\quad D^{\omega/}(n)=\frac{1}{n!}P^{(n)}_{\omega/}(z)\quad\text{and}\quad D^{\omega-}(n)=\frac{1}{n!}P^{(n)}_{\omega-}(z). (These Maclaurin coefficients are closely related to the ones defined in equation ( 1.3 ), except that those prior coefficients also include the effect of the correction factors depending on a a .)
Note that (6.1) D ω − ( 0 ) = ∏ q ( 1 − 1 q 2 − 1 ) , and that D Ω ( 0 ) = D ω / ( 0 ) = ∏ q ( 1 − 1 q ⁡ ( q − 1 ) ) D^{\omega-}(0)=\prod_{q}\biggl(1-\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\biggr),\quad\text{and that }D^{\Omega}(0)=D^{\omega/}(0)=\prod_{q}\biggl(1-\frac{1}{q(q-1)}\biggr) both equal Artin’s constant; Moree [ 10 ] provides precise numerical approximation of these constants. We derive recursive formulas (Proposition 6.4 below) for D Ω ​ ( n ) D^{\Omega}(n) , D ω / ( n ) D^{\omega/}(n) , and D ω − ​ ( n ) D^{\omega-}(n) for all n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , given in terms of certain quantities S ~ Ω ​ ( n ) \tilde{S}_{\Omega}(n) , S ~ ω / ( n ) \tilde{S}_{\omega/}(n) , and S ~ ω − ​ ( n ) \tilde{S}_{\omega-}(n) defined in equation ( 6.2 ) and evaluated in Lemma 6.5 . Proposition 6.4 . Proof. These are particular instances of Proposition 6.1 .
∎ The values of these coefficients for n = 0 n=0 are recorded in equation ( 6.1 ); to use these recursive formulas for the coefficients, it remains to exhibit formulas for the S ~ \tilde{S} quantities. Lemma 6.5 . For n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , g Ω ​ ( n , q , z ) \displaystyle g_{\Omega}(n,q,z) = ( n − 1 ) ! ( q 2 − z ) n − ( n − 1 ) ! A non-recursive formula Note that Proposition 6.1 gives a recursive formula for the C ⁡ ( n ) C(n) in terms of C ⁡ ( 0 ) C(0) and the S ~ ​ ( n ) \tilde{S}(n) . We take this opportunity to record an explicit (non-recursive) version, which can be derived using Faà di Bruno’s formula generalizing the chain rule. Proposition 6.7 . For n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , C ⁡ ( n ) = P ⁡ ( 0 ) ​ ∑ 0 ≤ m 1 , m 2 , … , m n ≤ n m 1 + 2 ​ m 2 + ⋯ + n ​ m n = n S ( 1 ) m 1 S ( 2 ) m 2 ⋯ S ( n ) m n m 1 ! m 2 ! ⋯ m n ! ⋅ 2 m 2 3 m 3 ⋯ n m n . C(n)=P(0)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq m_{1},m_{2},\dots,m_{n}\leq n\\
m_{1}+2m_{2}+\cdots+nm_{n}=n\end{subarray}}\frac{S(1)^{m_{1}}S(2)^{m_{2}}\cdots S(n)^{m_{n}}}{m_{1}!m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!\cdot 2^{m_{2}}3^{m_{3}}\cdots n^{m_{n}}}. Proof. We can check that B n , k ​ ( x 1 , … , x n ) B_{n,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}) depends only on its first n − k + 1 n-k+1 variables. We begin with Faà di Bruno’s formula for the high-order derivatives of a composition of two functions: d n d ​ z n ​ h ​ ( g ⁡ ( z ) ) = ∑ k = 1 n h ( k ) ​ ( g ⁡ ( z ) ) ​ B n , k ​ ( g ′ ​ ( z ) , g ′′ ​ ( z ) , … , g ( n ) ​ ( z ) ) . \frac{d^{n}}{dz^{n}}h\bigl(g(z)\bigr)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}h^{(k)}(g(z))B_{n,k}\bigl(g^{\prime}(z),g^{\prime\prime}(z),\dots,g^{(n)}(z)\bigr). ∎ 7. Decay rates and expectations Our generating functions allow us to gather even more information about the coefficients D a ω / ( n ) D_{a}^{\omega/}(n) , D a Ω ​ ( n ) D_{a}^{\Omega}(n) , and D a ω − ​ ( n ) D_{a}^{\omega-}(n) . The decay rates for these sequences of coefficients, as described in Corollary 1.14 , are proved in Section 7.1 ; the expectations of those sequences, which can be thought of as the average values of the statistics ω ⁡ ( ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) ) \omega\Bigl((p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)\Bigr) and Ω ⁡ ( ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) ) \Omega\Bigl((p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)\Bigr) and ω ⁡ ( p − 1 ) − ω ⁡ ( ord p ( a ) ) \omega(p-1)-\omega\Bigl(\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)\Bigr) when p p is chosen at random, are determined in Section 7.2 . 7.1. Decay rates We begin with a precise statement of the well-known phenomenon that the size of the Maclaurin coefficients of an analytic function is determined by the distance from the origin to the nearest singularity of the function. Lemma 7.1 . Let F ⁡ ( z ) = ∑ n = 0 ∞ a n ​ z n F(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n} be a meromorphic function on a neighbourhood of the closed disk of radius R R centred at the origin. Suppose that there are two complex numbers z 1 z_{1} and z 2 z_{2} with 0 < | z 1 | < | z 2 | = R 0<|z_{1}|<|z_{2}|=R such that F ⁡ ( z ) F(z) has simple poles at z = z 1 z=z_{1} and z = z 2 z=z_{2} , with residues r 1 r_{1} and r 2 r_{2} respectively, and no other poles in { | z | ≤ R } \{|z|\leq R\} . Then a n = − r 1 ​ z 1 − n − 1 + O F ​ ( R − n ) a_{n}=-r_{1}z_{1}^{-n-1}+O_{F}(R^{-n}) . Proof. If we define G ⁡ ( z ) = F ⁡ ( z ) − r 1 / ( z − z 1 ) − r 2 / ( z − z 2 ) G(z)=F(z)-r_{1}/(z-z_{1})-r_{2}/(z-z_{2}) , then G ⁡ ( z ) G(z) is analytic on a neighbourhood of { | z | ≤ R } \{|z|\leq R\} . When we write G ⁡ ( z ) = ∑ n = 0 ∞ b n ​ z n G(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}z^{n} , the standard Cauchy integral formula calculation gives b n = G ( n ) n ! = 1 2 ​ π ​ i ∮ | z | = R G ⁡ ( z ) z n + 1 d z ≪ G ∮ | z | = R 1 R n + 1 d | z | ≪ G R − n . b_{n}=\frac{G^{(n)}}{n!}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|z|=R}\frac{G(z)}{z^{n+1}}\,dz\ll_{G}\oint_{|z|=R}\frac{1}{R^{n+1}}\,d|z|\ll_{G}R^{-n}. But F ⁡ ( z ) = G ⁡ ( z ) − r 1 / z 1 ​ ( 1 − z / z 1 ) − r 2 / z 2 ​ ( 1 − z / z 2 ) F(z)=G(z)-r_{1}/z_{1}(1-z/z_{1})-r_{2}/z_{2}(1-z/z_{2}) means that ∑ n = 0 ∞ a n z n = − r 1 z 1 ∑ n = 0 ∞ ( z z 1 ) n − r 2 z 2 ∑ n = 0 ∞ ( z z 2 ) n + ∑ n = 0 ∞ b n z n , \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}=-\frac{r_{1}}{z_{1}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\biggl(\frac{z}{z_{1}}\biggr)^{n}-\frac{r_{2}}{z_{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\biggl(\frac{z}{z_{2}}\biggr)^{n}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}z^{n}, and therefore a n = − r 1 z 1 ​ ( 1 z 1 ) n − r 2 z 2 ​ ( 1 z 2 ) n + b n = − r 1 z 1 n + 1 + O F ​ ( R − n + 1 ) + O F ​ ( R − n ) a_{n}=-\frac{r_{1}}{z_{1}}\biggl(\frac{1}{z_{1}}\biggr)^{n}-\frac{r_{2}}{z_{2}}\biggl(\frac{1}{z_{2}}\biggr)^{n}+b_{n}=-\frac{r_{1}}{z_{1}^{n+1}}+O_{F}(R^{-{n+1}})+O_{F}(R^{-n}) as claimed.
∎ This lemma is relevant to the generating function appearing in Theorem 5.7 , whose poles we take some care to describe exactly. Lemma 7.2 . The main term in equation ( 5.4 ) is a meromorphic function on all of ℂ {\mathbb{C}} , with at most simple poles at z = q 2 z=q^{2} for every prime q q and no other poles. Proof. We refer freely to the notation from Definition 5.6 . The main term in question includes the product ∏ q ( 1 + ( z − 1 ) ​ q ( q − 1 ) ​ ( q 2 − z ) ) = ∏ q ( 1 + z − q q 2 ​ ( q − 1 ) ) / ∏ q ( 1 − z q 2 ) . \prod_{q}\biggl(1+\frac{(z-1)q}{(q-1)(q^{2}-z)}\biggr)=\prod_{q}\biggl(1+\frac{z-q}{q^{2}(q-1)}\biggr)\bigg/\prod_{q}\biggl(1-\frac{z}{q^{2}}\biggr). Since ∑ q 1 / q ⁡ ( q − 1 ) \sum_{q}1/q(q-1) and ∑ q 1 / q 2 \sum_{q}1/q^{2} converge, both products converge absolutely and uniformly on compact sets and are therefore entire functions; therefore their quotient is a meromorphic function on all of ℂ {\mathbb{C}} with at most simple poles at z = q 2 z=q^{2} for all primes q q . We also note that H h Ω ​ ( z ) \displaystyle H^{\Omega}_{h}(z) ∏ q ( 1 + ( z − 1 ) ​ q ( q − 1 ) ​ ( q 2 − z ) ) = H h Ω ​ ( z ) ​ ∏ q q 3 − q 2 − q + z ( q − 1 ) ​ ( q 2 − z ) \displaystyle\prod_{q}\biggl(1+\frac{(z-1)q}{(q-1)(q^{2}-z)}\biggr)=H^{\Omega}_{h}(z)\prod_{q}\frac{q^{3}-q^{2}-q+z}{(q-1)(q^{2}-z)} (7.1) = ∏ q | h ( q 4 − 2 ​ q 3 + 2 ​ q ​ z − z 2 − ( z − 1 ) ​ ( q − 1 ) ​ z ν q ​ ( h ) ​ q − ν q ​ ( h ) + 2 ( q − z ) ​ ( q − 1 ) ​ ( q 2 − z ) ) ​ ∏ q ∤ h q 3 − q 2 − q + z ( q − 1 ) ​ ( q 2 − z ) \displaystyle=\prod_{q\mid h}\biggl(\frac{q^{4}-2q^{3}+2qz-z^{2}-(z-1)(q-1)z^{\nu_{q}(h)}q^{-\nu_{q}(h)+2}}{(q-z)(q-1)(q^{2}-z)}\biggr)\prod_{q\nmid h}\frac{q^{3}-q^{2}-q+z}{(q-1)(q^{2}-z)} has no additional poles—the factor q − z q-z in the first denominator on the right-hand side does not produce a pole since the corresponding numerator vanishes at z = q z=q . Next we consider what happens when equation ( 7.1 ) is multiplied by the factor I h Ω ​ ( γ , z ) I_{h}^{\Omega}(\gamma,z) that is present in both cases of F a Ω ​ ( γ , z ) F_{a}^{\Omega}(\gamma,z) . Again both its numerator and denominator vanish to first order at z = q z=q , so no pole will be introduced there. For any q | γ q\mid\gamma , if q | h q\mid h as well then the denominator of I h Ω ​ ( γ , z ) I_{h}^{\Omega}(\gamma,z) is canceled by the identical numerator of H h Ω ​ ( γ , z ) H_{h}^{\Omega}(\gamma,z) ; on the other hand, if q ∤ h q\nmid h then the denominator of I h Ω ​ ( γ , z ) I_{h}^{\Omega}(\gamma,z) equals ( q − z ) ​ ( q 3 − q 2 − q + z ) (q-z)(q^{3}-q^{2}-q+z) , and the second factor is canceled by the second numerator on the right-hand side of equation ( 7.1 ). We now consider what happens when equation ( 7.1 ) is multiplied by the first fraction in the definition of F a Ω ​ ( h , z ) F_{a}^{\Omega}(h,z) . When 2 | h 2\mid h , its denominator is identical to the numerator of the q = 2 q=2 term in the definition of H h Ω ​ ( z ) H_{h}^{\Omega}(z) and is thus canceled. (Note that 2 2 never divides γ \gamma by definition, since b 0 b_{0} is squarefree, and so we are not using this numerator to cancel two different denominators.) On the other hand, when 2 ∤ h 2\nmid h then its denominator equals 4 − z 2 = − ( z − 2 ) ​ ( z + 2 ) 4-z^{2}=-(z-2)(z+2) ; the z − 2 z-2 is canceled by the numerator above it, while the z + 2 z+2 is canceled by the numerator 2 3 − 2 2 − 2 + z = 2 + z 2^{3}-2^{2}-2+z=2+z of the q = 2 q=2 term in the infinite product in equation ( 7.1 ). We are almost done examining the various ways that poles might have been introduced when multplying equation ( 7.1 ) by F a Ω ​ ( h , z ) F_{a}^{\Omega}(h,z) to obtain the main term in equation ( 5.4 ). The functions τ ± Ω ​ ( a , z ) \tau_{\pm}^{\Omega}(a,z) are entire in all cases. Both occurrences of 1 / ( 4 − z ) 1/(4-z) in the definition of F a Ω ​ ( h , z ) F_{a}^{\Omega}(h,z) are canceled by the z − 4 z-4 factor in the large fraction. Finally, the term ( 1 − ( z 2 ) ν 2 ​ ( h ) ) / ( z − 2 ) (1-(\frac{z}{2})^{\nu_{2}(h)})/(z-2) in the second case of F a Ω ​ ( h , z ) F_{a}^{\Omega}(h,z) has a removable singularity at z = 2 z=2 . These observations collectively establish the lemma.
∎ Proof of Corollary 1.14 (a). By Lemma 7.2 , we may apply Lemma 7.1 to the main term in equation ( 5.4 ), whose Maclaurin coefficients are the D a Ω ​ ( n ) D_{a}^{\Omega}(n) . Since the other terms are all rational functions of z z , the logarithms of their values are ≪ log ⁡ z \ll\log z when z z is large and real, which does not change the growth rate of the right-hand side.
∎ Proof of Corollary 1.14 (b). It is a classical fact from complex analysis (see [ 2 , Definition 2.1.1 and Theorem 2.2.2] ) that the rate of growth of an entire function f ⁡ ( z ) = ∑ m = 0 ∞ b m ​ z m f(z)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}b_{m}z^{m} is connected to the decay rate of its Maclaurin coefficients b m b_{m} by lim sup r → ∞ log ⁡ log ⁡ ( max | z | = r ⁡ | f ⁡ ( z ) | ) log ⁡ r = lim sup m → ∞ b m ≠ 0 m ​ log ⁡ m log ⁡ ( 1 / | b m | ) \limsup_{r\to\infty}\frac{\log\log\bigl(\max_{|z|=r}|f(z)|\bigr)}{\log r}=\limsup_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\to\infty\\
b_{m}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{m\log m}{\log(1/|b_{m}|)} (both sides, if finite, equal the “order” of f ⁡ ( z ) f(z) ). It is straightforward to deduce the proposition from this identity and the growth rates in Lemma 7.3 .
(See [ 4 , Proof of Corollary 4] for the details of deriving such an upper bound for the coefficients of an analogous function, including the argument that the growth rate for z z real and positive is indeed the maximal growth rate on the circle | z | = r |z|=r .)
∎ 7.2. Expectations Our functions D a ω / ( n ; x ) D_{a}^{\omega/}(n;x) , D a ω − ​ ( n , x ) D_{a}^{\omega-}(n;x) , and D a Ω ​ ( n , x ) D_{a}^{\Omega}(n;x) (and their limits D a ω / ( n ) D_{a}^{\omega/}(n) , D a ω − ​ ( n ) D_{a}^{\omega-}(n) , and D a Ω ​ ( n ) D_{a}^{\Omega}(n) if they exist) represent proportions of primes having certain properties. We can view these proportions as probabilities associated with discrete random variables, and study the expectations of these random variables. Notation 7.4 . Write 𝔼 a ω / ( x ) \displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{a}^{\omega/}(x) = ∑ n = 0 ∞ n D a ω / ( n ; x ) , \displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}nD_{a}^{\omega/}(n;x), which is the expectation of random variable X X with Prob ( X = n ) = D a ω / ( n ; x ) (X=n)=D_{a}^{\omega/}(n;x) for all n ≥ 0 n\geq 0 .
(Technically, for this to be a random variable, in equation ( 1.3 ) we should be dividing not by π ⁡ ( x ) \pi(x) but by the number of primes p ≤ x p\leq x for which ν p ​ ( a ) = 0 \nu_{p}(a)=0 , which is π ​ ( x ) + O a ​ ( 1 ) \pi(x)+O_{a}(1) ; but we shall ignore this technicality as it is insignificant in the limit.)
Similarly define 𝔼 a ω − ​ ( x ) = ∑ n = 0 ∞ n ​ D a ω − ​ ( n , x ) and 𝔼 a Ω ​ ( x ) = ∑ n = 0 ∞ n ​ D a Ω ​ ( n , x ) . \displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{a}^{\omega-}(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}nD_{a}^{\omega-}(n;x)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{a}^{\Omega}(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}nD_{a}^{\Omega}(n;x). Furthermore, if the appropriate limits exist, define 𝔼 a ω / = ∑ n = 0 ∞ n D a ω / ( n ) , 𝔼 a ω − = ∑ n = 0 ∞ n D a ω − ( n ) , and 𝔼 a Ω = ∑ n = 0 ∞ n D a Ω ( n ) . \displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{a}^{\omega/}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}nD_{a}^{\omega/}(n),\quad\mathbb{E}_{a}^{\omega-}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}nD_{a}^{\omega-}(n),\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{a}^{\Omega}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}nD_{a}^{\Omega}(n). Unconditional bounds Our conditional results can actually be converted into unconditional bounds, of which Corollary 1.15 is an example. We concentrate on the first statistic ω ⁡ ( ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) ) \omega\Bigl((p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)\Bigr) , but all our results have immediate analogues for the other two statistics Ω ⁡ ( ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) ) \Omega\Bigl((p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)\Bigr) and ω ⁡ ( p − 1 ) − ω ⁡ ( ord p ( a ) ) \omega(p-1)-\omega\Bigl(\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)\Bigr) , with the same proofs. In the notation of Definition 5.3 , define the Maclaurin series coefficients D ~ a ω / ( n ) \tilde{D}_{a}^{\omega/}(n) by ∑ n = 0 ∞ D ~ a ω / ( n ) z n = F a ω / ( z ) H a ω / ( z ) ∏ q ( 1 + z − 1 q ⁡ ( q − 1 ) ) , \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\tilde{D}_{a}^{\omega/}(n)z^{n}=F^{\omega/}_{a}(z)H^{\omega/}_{a}(z)\prod_{q}\biggl(1+\frac{z-1}{q(q-1)}\biggr), so that Theorem 5.4 is the statement (conditional on GRH) that lim x → ∞ D a ω / ( n ; x ) = D ~ a ω / ( n ) \lim_{x\to\infty}D_{a}^{\omega/}(n;x)=\tilde{D}_{a}^{\omega/}(n) for all n ≥ 0 n\geq 0 . We can modify the proof to establish: Theorem 8.1 . Unconditionally, for all k ≥ 0 k\geq 0 , lim sup x → ∞ ∑ n = 0 k D a ω / ( n ; x ) ≤ ∑ n = 0 k D ~ a ω / ( n ) . \limsup_{x\to\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{k}D_{a}^{\omega/}(n;x)\leq\sum_{n=0}^{k}\tilde{D}_{a}^{\omega/}(n). Note that the first assertion of Corollary 1.15 is the special case k = 0 k=0 . Theorem 8.2 . Unconditionally, lim inf x → ∞ ∑ n = 0 ∞ n D a ω / ( n ; x ) \displaystyle\liminf_{x\to\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}nD_{a}^{\omega/}(n;x) ≥ d F ω / a d ​ z ​ ( 1 ) + d H ω / a d ​ z ​ ( 1 ) + ∑ q 1 q ⁡ ( q − 1 ) , \displaystyle\geq\frac{dF^{\omega/}_{a}}{dz}(1)+\frac{dH^{\omega/}_{a}}{dz}(1)+\sum\limits_{q}\frac{1}{q(q-1)}, where H a ω / ( z ) H^{\omega/}_{a}(z) and F a ω / ( z ) F^{\omega/}_{a}(z) are as in Definition 5.3 . The analogues of these two theorems for the other statistics appear in Theorems 8.8 and 8.11 below. For all k ∈ ℕ k\in{\mathbb{N}} and all ξ ≤ n \xi\leq n , we thus have the trivial inequality (8.1) # ⁡ { p ≤ x : ω ⁡ ( p − 1 ord p ( a ) ) ≤ k } \displaystyle\#\biggl\{p\leq x\colon\omega\biggl(\frac{p-1}{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)}\biggr)\leq k\biggr\} ≤ # ⁡ { p ≤ x : ω ξ ​ ( p − 1 ord p ( a ) ) ≤ k } . \displaystyle\leq\#\biggl\{p\leq x\colon\omega_{\xi}\biggl(\frac{p-1}{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a)}\biggr)\leq k\biggr\}. Unconditional asymptotics for suitable ξ \xi We have already seen (in the proof of Theorem 3.4 ) that versions of the prime ideal theorem can be seen as special cases of the Chebotarev density theorem. In particular, we may adapt an unconditional Chebotarev density theorem by Lagarias and Odlyzko [ 7 ] to obtain the following prime ideal theorem: Theorem 8.3 . Let K K be a number field with discriminant Δ K \Delta_{K} and [ K : ℚ ] = n K [K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=n_{K} . There exist absolute positive constants c 1 c_{1} and c 2 c_{2} such that the prime ideal counting function π K ​ ( x ) \pi_{K}(x) satisfies π K ( x ) = π ( x ) + O ( π ( x 1 − 1 / ( 4 ​ log ⁡ | Δ K | ) ) + π ( x 1 − c 1 ​ | Δ k | − 1 + 1 / n K ) + x exp ( − c 2 n K − 1 / 2 ( log x ) 1 / 2 ) ) \pi_{K}(x)=\pi(x)+{\mathit{O}}\Bigl(\pi(x^{1-1/(4\log|\Delta_{K}|)})+\pi(x^{1-c_{1}|\Delta_{k}|^{-1+1/n_{K}}})+x\exp(-c_{2}n_{K}^{-1/2}(\log x)^{1/2})\Bigr) uniformly in K K . We can then derive an unconditional version of Theorem 3.4 : Theorem 8.4 . Let a ∈ ℚ ∖ { − 1 , 0 , 1 } a\in{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus\{-1,0,1\} , and let m m and ℓ \ell be positive integers. ∎ Remark 8.7 . These error terms are certainly not best possible: with more work one should be able to obtain something similar to Wiertelak’s result [ 16 ] for the upper bound in the classical Artin’s conjecture setting. Other results on unconditional upper bounds are discussed in [ 9 ] as well. We state without further proof the analogous results for the other two statistics.

Theorem 8.8 . Unconditionally, for all k ≥ 0 k\geq 0 , lim sup x → ∞ ∑ n = 0 k D a Ω ​ ( n , x ) ≤ ∑ n = 0 k D ~ a Ω ​ ( n ) and lim sup x → ∞ ∑ n = 0 k D a ω − ​ ( n , x ) ≤ ∑ n = 0 k D ~ a ω − ​ ( n ) . \limsup_{x\to\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{k}D_{a}^{\Omega}(n;x)\leq\sum_{n=0}^{k}\tilde{D}_{a}^{\Omega}(n)\quad\text{and}\quad\limsup_{x\to\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{k}D_{a}^{\omega-}(n;x)\leq\sum_{n=0}^{k}\tilde{D}_{a}^{\omega-}(n). Note that the two assertions in Corollary 1.15 are the special case k = 0 k=0 of these two inequalities. Furthermore, these unconditional results lead to an unconditional lower bound for the expected number of prime factors of ( p − 1 ) / ord p ( a ) (p-1)/\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{p}(a) (and similarly for the other two statistics) described in Section 7.2 , which is the subject of the next section. 8.2. Lemma 8.10 . Let a n , b n ≥ 0 a_{n},b_{n}\geq 0 with ∑ n = 0 ∞ a n = ∑ n = 0 ∞ b n = 1 \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}=1 . Suppose that ∑ n = 0 j a n ≤ ∑ n = 0 j b n \sum_{n=0}^{j}a_{n}\leq\sum_{n=0}^{j}b_{n} for all j ≥ 0 j\geq 0 . Then 𝔼 ⁡ ( a n ) ≥ 𝔼 ⁡ ( b n ) \mathbb{E}(a_{n})\geq\mathbb{E}(b_{n}) . Proof. We have (8.8) 𝔼 ⁡ ( a n ) = ∑ n = 0 ∞ n ​ a n = ∑ n = 0 ∞ a n ​ ∑ k = 1 n 1 = ∑ k = 1 ∞ ∑ n = k ∞ a n = ∑ k = 1 ∞ ( 1 − ∑ n = 0 k − 1 a n ) . \mathbb{E}(a_{n})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}na_{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}1=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}a_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\biggl(1-\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}a_{n}\biggr). Therefore 𝔼 ⁡ ( b n ) = ∑ k = 1 ∞ ( 1 − ∑ n = 0 k − 1 b n ) ≤ ∑ k = 1 ∞ ( 1 − ∑ n = 0 k − 1 a n ) = 𝔼 ⁡ ( a n ) . ∎ \mathbb{E}(b_{n})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\biggl(1-\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}b_{n}\biggr)\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\biggl(1-\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}a_{n}\biggr)=\mathbb{E}(a_{n}).\qed Proof of Theorem 8.1 . From the inequality ( 8.3 ) and Lemma 8.10 , (8.9) ∑ n = 0 ∞ n D a ω / ( n ; x ) ≥ ∑ n = 0 ∞ n D a , ξ ω / ( n ; x ) , \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}nD^{\omega/}_{a}(n;x)\geq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}nD^{\omega/}_{a,\xi}(n;x), and taking the lim inf \liminf on both sides and using Theorem 8.9 yields the result.
